    Headline
    hyperkalemia, nephrotoxicity, renal failure, renal papillary necrosis
    General Information
    Long-term administration of NSAIDs, such as meloxicam, can result in renal papillary necrosis, renal insufficiency, acute renal failure, and other renal injury. Nephrotoxicity has been noted in patients in whom renal prostaglandins have a compensatory role in the maintenance of renal perfusion. In these patients, NSAIDs may cause a dose-dependent reduction in prostaglandin formation, thereby reducing renal blood flow precipitating overt renal decompensation. Discontinuation of NSAID therapy is usually followed by recovery to the pretreatment state. Increases in serum potassium concentration, including hyperkalemia, have been reported with use of NSAIDs, even in some patients without renal impairment. In patients with normal renal function, these effects have been attributed to a hyporeninemic-hypoaldosteronism state.

    Headline
    constipation, dysgeusia, gastritis, GI bleeding, GI perforation, hematemesis, melena, nausea, peptic ulcer, vomiting
    General Information
    As with other NSAIDs, cases of serious, and sometimes fatal, GI bleeding, GI ulceration, or GI perforation have been reported in persons receiving meloxicam. Overall, GI ulcer, bleeding, or perforation occurs in approximately 1% of persons treated with NSAIDs for 3 to 6 months and in about 2% to 4% of persons treated for a year with trends continuing with longer durations of use. Gastrointestinal adverse reactions reported with bupivacaine; meloxicam in clinical trials and occurring with 5% or more incidence and higher than saline placebo include dysgeusia (9%), constipation (24%), nausea (50%), and vomiting (26%). Constipation and vomiting were among the most common adverse reactions (5% or more) after bupivacaine; meloxicam administration in subjects undergoing orthopedic procedures and soft tissue procedures, respectively. Peptic ulcer hemorrhage, gastritis requiring hospitalization, GI hemorrhage, hematemesis, and melena were observed in subjects undergoing soft tissue or orthopedic procedures who received bupivacaine; meloxicam as part of a scheduled, non-opioid multimodal analgesic regimen including 1 or more other NSAIDs (i.e., ibuprofen, ketorolac, and celecoxib) and had predisposing risk factors (i.e., comorbidities and/or concomitant medication such as anticoagulant and/or antiplatelet medications) that increase the risk for NSAID-related gastrointestinal toxicity.

    elevated hepatic enzymes, hepatic failure, hepatic necrosis, hepatitis, hepatotoxicity
    General Information
    Elevated hepatic enzymes (less than 3 times the upper limit of normal) may occur in up to 15% of persons taking NSAIDs, including meloxicam. Notable elevations of ALT or AST (3 times or more the upper limit of normal) have been reported in approximately 1% of subjects in clinical trials with NSAIDs. Rare, sometimes fatal, cases of severe hepatic injury, including fulminant hepatitis, hepatic necrosis, and hepatic failure have been reported with NSAIDs. Elevated hepatic enzymes were observed in subjects undergoing soft tissue or orthopedic procedures who received bupivacaine; meloxicam as part of a scheduled, non-opioid multimodal analgesic regimen including 1 or more other NSAIDs (i.e., ibuprofen, ketorolac, and celecoxib) and had predisposing risk factors (i.e., comorbidities and/or concomitant medication such as anticoagulant and/or antiplatelet medications) that increase the risk for NSAID-related gastrointestinal toxicity. The risk of these events after a single-dose local application of bupivacaine; meloxicam is uncertain. If clinical signs and symptoms consistent with hepatotoxicity develop or if systemic manifestations occur, promptly evaluate.

    anaphylactoid reactions, exfoliative dermatitis, fixed drug eruption, hyperhidrosis, pruritus, Stevens-Johnson syndrome, toxic epidermal necrolysis
    General Information
    NSAIDs can cause serious and potentially fatal skin reactions, including exfoliative dermatitis, Stevens-Johnson syndrome (SJS), toxic epidermal necrolysis (TEN), and fixed drug eruption (FDE), which may present as a more severe variant known as generalized bullous fixed drug eruption (GBFDE). These serious events may occur without warning. Inform individuals about the signs and symptoms of serious skin reactions and instruct them to discontinue use of the NSAID at the first appearance of skin rash or any other sign of hypersensitivity. Meloxicam has been associated with anaphylactoid reactions. Advise individuals to seek emergency help if an anaphylactic reaction occurs.     Headline
    Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS)
    General Information
    Drug Reaction with Eosinophilia and Systemic Symptoms (DRESS), a multi-organ hypersensitivity reaction, has occurred with NSAIDs. Some of these events have been life-threatening or fatal. DRESS typically presents as fever, rash, and/or lymphadenopathy in conjunction with other organ system involvement including hepatitis, nephritis, hematologic abnormalities, myocarditis, or myositis sometimes resembling an acute viral infection. Eosinophilia is often present. Early manifestations such as fever and lymphadenopathy may be present without evidence of a rash. If such symptoms occur, evaluate the patient immediately and treat as clinically indicated.

    General Information
    The pharmacological activity of NSAIDs in reducing inflammation, and possibly fever, may result in diagnostic test interference by diminishing the utility of these diagnostic signs in detecting infection.

    Headline
    infertility
    General Information
    NSAIDs may delay or prevent prostaglandin-mediated rupture of ovarian follicles, which has been associated with reversible infertility. Small studies with NSAIDs demonstrated a reversible delay in ovulation. Consider withdrawal of NSAIDs in people who may become pregnant who have difficulties conceiving or who are undergoing infertility evaluation. Also, meloxicam may impair male fertility. Oral administration of meloxicam to male rats for 35 days resulted in decreased sperm count and motility and histopathological evidence of testicular degeneration at 0.8 times the maximum recommended human dose based on body surface area comparison. It is not known if these effects on fertility are reversible. The clinical relevance of these findings is unknown.

Description
  Text
  Bupivacaine; meloxicam is a combination of an amide local anesthetic and nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID) indicated for postsurgical local analgesia for up to 72 hours after soft tissue or orthopedic surgical procedures, including foot and ankle procedures or other orthopedic surgical procedures in which direct exposure to articular cartilage is avoided. In clinical trials, bupivacaine; meloxicam significantly reduced postoperative pain intensity and opioid use for up to 72 hours compared to bupivacaine alone or placebo. Safety and efficacy have not been established in highly vascular surgeries, such as intrathoracic, large 4 or more level spinal, and head and neck procedures. The safety and effectiveness of local anesthetics depend on proper dosage, correct technique, adequate precautions, and readiness for emergencies. Meloxicam, like all NSAIDs, may exacerbate hypertension and congestive heart failure and may cause an increased risk of serious cardiovascular thromboembolism, acute myocardial infarction, and stroke, which can be fatal. Also, serious gastrointestinal (GI) tract adverse effects, such as GI bleeding or inflammation and stomach or other GI perforation and ulceration, can occur without warning or symptoms in patients receiving NSAIDs.

Mechanism Of Action
  Text
  Like all local anesthetics, bupivacaine causes a reversible nerve-conduction blockade by decreasing nerve membrane permeability to sodium. This decreases the rate of membrane depolarization, thereby increasing the threshold for electrical excitability. Clinically, loss of nerve function occurs in the order of 1) pain, 2) temperature, 3) touch, 4) proprioception, and 5) skeletal muscle tone. Meloxicam inhibits prostaglandin synthesis, primarily by competitive inhibition of both cyclooxygenase (COX) isoenzymes, COX-1 and COX-2, resulting in analgesic and antiinflammatory pharmacologic effects. Prostaglandins sensitize afferent nerves and potentiate the action of bradykinin in inducing pain. Prostaglandins are mediators of inflammation. Meloxicam may decrease prostaglandins in peripheral tissues.

Pharmacokinetics
  Route Specific Pharmacokinetics
    Name
    Other Route(s)
    Description
    Soft Tissue or Periarticular Instillation Route
The rate of systemic absorption of bupivacaine or meloxicam from the instillation site is dependent upon the total dose of drug administered and the vascularity of the site. The highest individual maximum plasma concentration (Cmax) of bupivacaine was 1,830 ng/mL after administration of 400 mg bupivacaine/12 mg meloxicam by instillation. After a single dose of 300 mg bupivacaine/9 mg meloxicam in subjects who underwent herniorrhaphy (n = 16), pharmacokinetic parameters for bupivacaine and meloxicam, respectively, were Cmax = 271 and 225 ng/mL, Tmax = 18 and 54 hours, AUC (0 to 120 hours post-dose) = 15,174 and 18,721 hours x ng/mL, and half-life = 16 hours for bupivacaine while not reported for meloxicam. After a single dose of 400 mg bupivacaine/12 mg meloxicam in subjects who underwent abdominoplasty (n = 22), pharmacokinetic parameters for bupivacaine and meloxicam, respectively, were Cmax = 382 and 116 ng/mL, Tmax = 31 and 24 hours, AUC (0 to 144 hours post-dose) = 24,411 and 7,924 hours x ng/mL, and half-life = 20 and 21 hours. After a single dose of 400 mg bupivacaine/12 mg meloxicam in subjects who underwent augmentation mammoplasty (n = 49), pharmacokinetic parameters for bupivacaine and meloxicam, respectively, were Cmax = 710 and 527 ng/mL, Tmax = 3.6 and 20 hours, AUC (0 to 120 hours post-dose) = 27,363 and 30,499 hours x ng/mL, and half-life = 25 and 42 hours. After a single dose of 400 mg bupivacaine/12 mg meloxicam in subjects who underwent Cesarean section (n = 11), pharmacokinetic parameters for bupivacaine and meloxicam, respectively, were Cmax = 291 and 114 ng/mL, Tmax = 24 and 60 hours, AUC (0 to 120 hours post-dose) = 17,923 and 9,710 hours x ng/mL, and half-life = 10 and 21 hours. After a single dose of 60 mg bupivacaine/1.8 mg meloxicam in subjects who underwent bunionectomy (n = 17), pharmacokinetic parameters for bupivacaine and meloxicam, respectively, were Cmax = 54 and 26 ng/mL, Tmax = 3 and 18 hours, AUC (0 to 120 hours post-dose) = 1,681 and 1,621 hours x ng/mL, and half-life = 15 and 33 hours. After a single dose of 400 mg bupivacaine/12 mg meloxicam in subjects who underwent total knee arthroplasty (n = 53), pharmacokinetic parameters for bupivacaine and meloxicam, respectively, were Cmax = 695 and 275 ng/mL, Tmax = 21 and 36 hours, AUC (0 to 144 hours post-dose) = 35,889 and 19,525 hours x ng/mL, and half-life = 17 and 42 hours. After a single dose of 400 mg bupivacaine/12 mg meloxicam in subjects who underwent total shoulder arthroplasty (n = 20), pharmacokinetic parameters for bupivacaine and meloxicam, respectively, were Cmax = 372 and 248 ng/mL, Tmax = 20 and 57 hours, AUC (0 to 144 hours post-dose) = 21,132 and 22,292 hours x ng/mL, and half-life = 10 and 29 hours. After a single dose of bupivacaine 92 mg/2.8 mg meloxicam in subjects who underwent 1-level spinal surgery (n = 13), pharmacokinetic parameters for bupivacaine and meloxicam, respectively, were Cmax = 95 and 53 ng/mL, Tmax = 23 and 18 hours, AUC (0 to 120 hours post-dose) = 4,467 and 3,556 hours x ng/mL, and half-life = 25 and 35 hours. After a single dose of 191 mg bupivacaine/5.7 mg meloxicam in subjects who underwent 2- or 3-level spinal surgery (n = 13), pharmacokinetic parameters for bupivacaine and meloxicam, respectively, were Cmax = 163 and 90 ng/mL, Tmax = 24 and 43 hours, AUC (0 to 120 hours post-dose) = 8,488 and 6,269 hours x ng/mL, and half-life = 14 and 38 hours. When administered at the same dose, respectively, the pharmacokinetics of bupivacaine; meloxicam are not expected to be significantly different when used in procedures with similar surgical site characteristics, tissue type, surgical area size, and vascularity.
  Special Populations
    Name
    Hepatic Impairment
    Description
    After bupivacaine and meloxicam have been released from the extended-release solution and are absorbed systemically, the effects of hepatic impairment are expected to be the same as for other bupivacaine and meloxicam formulations. Hepatic disease may decrease the ability of the liver to metabolize bupivacaine. Persons with severe hepatic disease may be more susceptible to developing toxic bupivacaine plasma concentrations. Hepatic impairment may alter the disposition of meloxicam. The clinical significance of this effect is not known. In subjects with mild (Child-Pugh Class I) and moderate (Child-Pugh Class II) hepatic impairment, there are no marked differences in meloxicam plasma concentrations compared to healthy volunteers. Protein binding of meloxicam is not affected by hepatic insufficiency. Persons with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class III) have not been adequately studied.
    Name
    Renal Impairment
    Description
    After bupivacaine and meloxicam have been released from the extended-release solution and are absorbed systemically, the effects of renal impairment are expected to be the same as for other bupivacaine and meloxicam formulations. Bupivacaine is renally excreted. Urinary excretion is affected by urinary perfusion and factors affecting pH. Renal impairment may alter the disposition of meloxicam. The clinical significance of this effect is not known. In persons with renal insufficiency, total meloxicam plasma concentrations decreased according to the degree of renal impairment, while the free AUC values are similar. Meloxicam total clearance is likely greater in renally impaired persons due to an increase in the unbound fraction of meloxicam. Persons with severe renal insufficiency have not been adequately studied. The unbound peak meloxicam plasma concentrations are higher in persons with end-stage renal failure receiving chronic hemodialysis (1% unbound fraction) in comparison to healthy volunteers (0.3% unbound fraction). Hemodialysis does not significantly remove meloxicam from the circulation.
    Name
    Geriatric
    Description
    Age does not have a clinically meaningful effect on the pharmacokinetics of bupivacaine and meloxicam in the extended-release solution.
    Name
    Gender Differences
    Description
    Sex does not have a clinically meaningful effect on the pharmacokinetics of bupivacaine and meloxicam in the extended-release solution.
    Name
    Ethnic Differences
    Description
    Race and ethnicity do not have a clinically meaningful effect on the pharmacokinetics of bupivacaine and meloxicam in the extended-release solution.
    Name
    Other
    Description
    CYP2C9 Poor Metabolizers
Limited data show that meloxicam AUC was substantially higher in individuals with reduced CYP2C9 activity, particularly in poor metabolizers (e.g., *3/*3), compared to normal metabolizers (*1/*1).
  Text
  Bupivacaine; meloxicam is administered by instillation to the surgical site. The rate of absorption of bupivacaine or meloxicam from bupivacaine; meloxicam is dependent upon the total dose of drug administered and the vascularity of the administration site. After bupivacaine and meloxicam have been released from the extended-release solution and are absorbed systemically, their distribution and excretion are expected to be the same as for other bupivacaine solution formulations or meloxicam oral formulations. Systemic plasma concentrations of bupivacaine and meloxicam after application of bupivacaine; meloxicam do not correlate with local efficacy. Bupivacaine is distributed to some extent to all tissues, with a high concentration in well-perfused organs such as the liver, lung, heart, and brain. Bupivacaine is highly protein-bound (95% in adults), particularly to alpha1-acid glycoprotein. Bupivacaine is metabolized primarily in the liver via conjugation with glucuronic acid. Additionally, bupivacaine is N-dealkylated by the CYP3A subfamily, CYP2C19, and CYP2D6 to form pipecolylxylidine, the major metabolite of bupivacaine. Pipecolylxylidine is hydroxylated to form glucuronide conjugates. Approximately 6% of the dose is excreted in the urine unchanged; urinary excretion is affected by urinary perfusion and pH. The mean apparent terminal half-life for bupivacaine from the extended-release solution is approximately 14 to 15 hours. Meloxicam is about 99.4% bound to human plasma proteins, primarily to albumin, and is about 99% bound in persons with renal disease. The free fraction in synovial fluid is 2.5 times higher than in plasma due to the lower albumin content in synovial fluid as compared to plasma; the significance of this penetration is unknown. Meloxicam is extensively hepatically metabolized to 4 pharmacologically inactive metabolites. The major metabolites are formed by hydroxylation and further oxidation of the methyl group of the thiazolyl moiety. Specifically, 5'-carboxy meloxicam (60% of dose), is formed by oxidation of the intermediate metabolite, 5'-hydroxymethyl meloxicam, which is itself excreted (9% of dose). In vitro studies indicate that CYP2C9 enzymes are primarily involved in this metabolic pathway, with a minor contribution by CYP3A4 enzymes. Peroxidase activity is probably responsible for the other 2 metabolites which account for 16% and 4% of the administered dose, respectively. Meloxicam excretion is predominantly in the form of metabolites, with 43% of the dose excreted in the urine and remainder appearing in the feces. Only traces of unchanged drug are excreted in the urine (0.2%) and feces (1.6%). About 6% and 13% of the dose is found in urine in the form of the 5'-hydroxymethyl and 5'-carboxy metabolites, respectively. The mean apparent terminal half-life for meloxicam from the extended-release solution is approximately 22 to 25 hours.
 
Affected cytochrome P450 isoenzymes and drug transporters: CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP3A4
Formation of pipecolylxylidine, the primary metabolite of bupivacaine, appears to be primarily mediated by CYP3A4. The isoenzymes CYP2C19 and CYP2D6 may play minor roles in degradation. Pipecolylxylidine constitutes 5% of the dose, and thus N-dealkylation does not appear to account for a large percentage of the drug's metabolism. Drug interactions related to CYP3A4 inhibition are unlikely. In vitro studies indicate that CYP2C9 enzymes are primarily involved in the major metabolic pathway of meloxicam, with a minor contribution by CYP3A4 enzymes.

Administration
  General Information
  For storage information, see the specific product information within the How Supplied section.
  Route Specific Administrations
    Name
    Other Administration Route(s)
    Description
    Do not administer bupivacaine; meloxicam by epidural, intrathecal, intravascular, or intra-articular routes, as regional nerve block, or pre-incisional or pre-procedural locoregional anesthetic techniques.
Soft Tissue or Periarticular Administration
Visually inspect the product for particulate matter and discoloration prior to administration whenever solution and container permit; the solution is a clear, pale yellow to yellow, viscous liquid.
As there is a potential risk of severe, life-threatening adverse reactions associated with the administration of bupivacaine, administer bupivacaine; meloxicam in a setting where trained personnel and equipment are available to promptly treat individuals who show evidence of neurologic or cardiac toxicity.
Avoid additional local anesthetic administration within 96 hours after bupivacaine; meloxicam administration. If additional local anesthetic administration cannot be avoided based on clinical need, monitor for neurologic and cardiovascular effects related to local anesthetic systemic toxicity.
The safety of additional NSAID administration during treatment has not been evaluated. Monitor for NSAID toxicity if additional NSAID medication is indicated during the postoperative period.
Use is compatible with surgical mesh materials, including polypropylene (Prolene), Gore-tex, and polyester, silicone membranes, bone cement, and metal alloys used in surgical implants.
The solution does not degrade sutures. When using monofilament sutures, 3 or more knots ending in a multi-throw knot (e.g., a Surgeon's knot) are recommended, as contact with the solution may cause a single knot to loosen or untie. Braided or barbed sutures are recommended, especially for closure of deeper layers.
Storage: Vials are for single dose only. Discard any unused product.
 
Preparation
Do not dilute. Do not mix with water, saline, or other local anesthetics as the product will become more viscous and difficult to administer.
Prepare vial for filling of syringe(s) by attaching vented vial spike. Prepare syringe by filling with air, then attach to vented vial spike.
Invert vial to allow product to fill the vial neck and push air into the vial. Withdraw dose of bupivacaine; meloxicam into provided syringe(s) immediately prior to use. Apply syringe tip cap until product delivery.
 
Administration
When a topical antiseptic is applied, allow the surgical site to dry before the local anesthetic is applied.
Remove the syringe tip cap and attach the Luer lock cone-shaped applicator to the syringe.
Using the Luer lock cone-shaped applicator only, apply the solution to tissues within the surgical site after implant placement (if applicable), after final irrigation and suctioning, and prior to suturing of each layer, when multiple tissue layers are involved.
For abdominal procedures, apply after closure of the peritoneum, if applicable, and avoid administration below the peritoneum.
For total joint arthroplasty, apply directly into the joint capsule, onto the periosteum, and the antero-, medial-, and lateral tissues, if applicable, after placement of the component(s).
For spinal procedures, apply after closure of the paraspinal musculature and again after closure of the subcutaneous fascia. Do not apply to the dura or spinal cord.
For soft tissue procedures, apply into the wound prior to closure of each layer within the surgical space.
For orthopedic procedures, apply into the wound and on the periosteum from the proximal to the distal ends (i.e., beyond the bony repair) of the wound.
Only apply the solution to the tissue layers below the skin incision and not directly onto the subdermal layer or the skin.
Minimize administration of the solution near the incision line. Wipe off excess solution from the skin prior to or during wound closure.
Use only the amount of solution necessary to coat the tissues to avoid leaking from the surgical wound after closure. The solution becomes more viscous during contact with moisture in tissues, which allows the product to stay in place.

Dosage Adjustment Guidelines
  Hepatic Impairment
  Use bupivacaine; meloxicam in patients with severe hepatic impairment only if the benefits are expected to outweigh the risks; monitor patients for signs of worsening liver function. Consider increased monitoring for local anesthetic systemic toxicity in patients with moderate to severe hepatic disease. No dose adjustment of bupivacaine; meloxicam is necessary in patients with mild to moderate hepatic impairment.
  Renal Impairment
  The use of bupivacaine; meloxicam in patients with severe renal impairment is not recommended. Consider reducing the bupivacaine; meloxicam dose in patients with mild to moderate renal impairment.
 
Intermittent hemodialysis
Meloxicam is not dialyzable. When using bupivacaine; meloxicam in patients on hemodialysis do not exceed maximum recommended dose or use with other meloxicam-containing products.
